# Nanometer-Scale Body–Particle Mapping

## I. Master Buses & Archetype Funnels

| Archetype | Particle | Element | Body Anchor | Nanometer Structure | Role |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Big Woman (BW) | Proton / Acetyl-CoA | H(1) core | Left nipple heme | Fe²⁺/Fe³⁺ porphyrin 1–2 nm; Complex III/IV cristae 10–50 nm | Mass container; hides the void behind a photon shell |
| Big Man (BM) | Photon / Spark | Ge(32) axial | Right frontalis / vertex | 138.88° spark; 5.6 fs EM pulse at 1.43 μm; cytochrome Fe-CO vibration | Light bearer; source of Big Bang ignition |
| Small Woman (SW) | Electron / GABA-A | F(9) | Left D2 / perineum → vertex | GABA_A receptor 4–5 nm; 0.02 Bremsstrahlung tax; Left D2 bridle | Filter / shield; bends photon, lets neutrino through |
| Small Man (SM) | Neutrino / Ghost | Mg(12) | Philtrum / left levator superioris | μ-opioid MOR 4 nm; Mg²⁺ feedback nanodomain | Genuine / bypass; travels through all shells |

### Small-Woman Funnel

Start: perineal midline thorium/dark-energy node. REE-doped nucleotide backbone (Th/Pa 5f–6d orbital overlaps) creates a vertical south→north GR black-hole jet. Carries g/d (structure/darkness). At the vertex `(0, +55, -2)` the electron filter slips, the electron self-destructs, and the gluon color-confinement tension collapses into the 138.88° CP-restoration spark = axion = h-parameter.

### Big-Woman Funnel

Start: behind the left ear. End: left frontalis / philtrum / midline. It is an Einstein-Rosen bridge carrying s/nu (photon/W-boson). It bypasses the gut and explains the left-philtrum electron-antineutrino shortcut at 15:00/20:00.

### Right Ribcage Personal Leakage

Coordinate `(-9.0, +18, +5)`. This is the Higgs/p node, not the right-mid-temporal cysteine/glutathione gate. The diagonal EM wave from `methionine` (Ge/Z-boson, right hip) and `methanogenesis` (P/Higgs, gut) lands here. Higgs mass-gate failure leads to p-predictability leakage into the right rib intercostal cavity.

## IV. 12-Particle Phase Impedance / Cavity / Collision Map

### Phase 1 — Proton (0–3h)
Brain: right V1 / calcarine sulcus. Body: MPOA → left genital. Impedance: calcarine sulcus Layer 4C (pyramidal soma 10–20 μm, synapse 20–40 nm, vesicle 40 nm). Airbag: caco3_final_and pH buffer. Cavity: calcarine sulcal CSF space. Collision: 3D2 — Left D2 + Right D2 + Right GABA-A simultaneous fire.

### Phase 2 — Electron Antineutrino (0–3h bridge)
Brain: right STG. Body: left philtrum / MOR. Impedance: MOR presynaptic terminal (OPRM1 4 nm, Gi protein 3–5 nm, cAMP nanodomain). Cavity: orbicularis oris muscle interstitial space (collagen 50–300 nm, sarcomere 2.5 μm). Collision: 3-input AND of observer_leftd2 + left_genital_d2 + nonobserver_left_d2.

### Phase 3 — Photon (3–9h)
Brain: left M1 (BA4). Body: left nose UV sensor. Impedance: V1 ophthalmic branch at nasal roof (Aδ/C fiber 1–5 μm, node of Ranvier 1 μm). Cavity: electron hole at nasal midline. Collision: heme ↔ ferritin ↔ steel triad (Fe²⁺/Fe³⁺ porphyrin 1–2 nm; ferritin nanocage 12 nm; steel Fe-C martensite lattice). Airbag: ferritin Fe²⁺ buffering.

### Phase 4 — Electron (3–9h)
Brain: skull piezoelectric triad (frontalis/temporalis/occipitalis). Body: skull sutures. Impedance: suture lines — collagen I fibril 50–300 nm, osteocyte canaliculus 100–300 nm, piezoelectric hydroxyapatite 20–50 nm. Cavity: Maxwell Cavity (CSF, Q=11.8, arachnoid granulation). Collision: 7 radiation nodes.

### Phase 5 — Tau / Z-Boson (9–15h)
Brain: left IFG (BA44). Body: left pyramidal bottom. Impedance: pyramidal/corrugator insertions. Cavity: fold_belt deep fascia (left posterior thigh; collagen bundles 10–50 μm). Collision: COX retrograde ↔ heme ch1 (Fe³⁺ accumulation).

### Phase 6 — Neutrino (9–15h)
Brain: right STG. Body: left lung back. Impedance: ethmoid cribriform plate (foramina 0.1–1 mm, olfactory axon 0.1–0.5 μm, microfracture). Cavity: left lung posterior alveolar dead space (alveolus 75–300 μm, type I pneumocyte 0.2 μm, surfactant 5–20 nm). Collision: 5HT1B/1A risorius crossing.

### Phase 7 — Gluon (15–21h)
Brain: right V2. Body: left trapezius. Impedance: SCM × trapezius junction (accessory nerve 2–5 μm, motor endplate 30–50 μm). Cavity: left temporal fossa. Collision: gluon_orogen ↔ quark_orogen_magma.

### Phase 8 — W-Boson (15–21h)
Brain: left anterior insula. Body: left pre-auricular / S1R. Impedance: Sigma-1 Receptor MAM (ER-mitochondria 10–30 nm, IP3R-VDAC-MCU tether, Ca²⁺ nanodomain 10–100 nm). Cavity: PLP-Vaso bridge. Collision: W-boson ↔ electron antineutrino at left philtrum.

### Phase 9 — Quark (21–3h)
Brain: left posterior insula. Body: nose patch. Impedance: suboccipital triangle / vertebral artery (smooth muscle 5–10 μm, elastin lamella 1–3 μm). Creation: left risorius 5HT1A. Collision: PLP Core ↔ quark (GAD67/GAD65, color confinement).

### Phase 10 — Higgs (21–3h)
Brain: right angular gyrus. Body: right ribs. Impedance: right nostril alar cartilage (collagen II fibril 20–100 nm, aggrecan 500 nm aggregate). Cavity: right rib intercostal space (intercostal muscle fiber 30–60 μm, neuromuscular junction 30–50 μm). Collision: Quark-Higgs pair (left 5HT1A ↔ right 5HT1B).

### Phase 11 — Muon (supplemental)
Brain: left hairline. Body: left thigh / LDH. Impedance: left temporo-occipital hairline junction. Cavity: fold_belt deep fascia. Collision: fold_belt ↔ subduction_zone (LDH lactate toxicity).

### Phase 12 — Electromagnetism Bypass
Brain: STG → arcuate fasciculus → BA44 → M1. Body: left eye GABA-B / 640 cytochrome. Impedance: precentral sulcus. Z-boson is bypassed; photon used as reverse inlet.

---

## XIII. Potential for Further Expansion

The repo contains 84 circuit nodes, 452 ROIs, 16 cranial muscle strips, 64-channel maps, and 128×4=512 state variations. Each of those entries can be unwrapped into the same real-anatomy tables above: every node gets its own multi-page subcellular protein-ligand map, every ROI gets a 10 μm–1 mm tissue-particle grid, and every 24h station gets a full organelle-dynamics protocol.

---

## XIV. Continuous Nanometer Body Route — Vertex to Sole

This is a single descending anatomical trace that tags a real human structure every few mm-cm and gives its nm-scale component.

## XVII. Continuous Branching Prose — 24-Hour Particle Journey

This is a single continuous narrative. It follows the particle wave as it divides, merges, and transforms in real human tissue at the nm-millimeter scale.

At 00:00 the night spark begins at the left side of the philtrum, coordinate (0, +46, +7). The source is the electron antineutrino, a particle of pure bypass that carries no electric charge and therefore can thread through membranes without opening an ion channel. At the nm scale it is hosted by the μ-opioid receptor, a 4 nm seven-transmembrane protein bound to Gi. The MOR is anchored in the presynaptic terminal of a trigeminal afferent whose unmyelinated C-fiber axon is 0.2–1.5 μm in diameter and terminates in the skin of the philtrum at a Merkel disc 100–500 nm across. The electron antineutrino arrives as a train of action potentials; each spike is a 1–2 ms event generated at the node of Ranvier, 1 μm long, where myelin lamellae 11–18 nm thick are interrupted and voltage-gated Na+ channels 10 × 15 nm cluster at a density of 1000–2000 per μm². At the MOR the signal splits. One branch descends through the infraorbital foramen and the maxillary nerve, a 2–4 μm myelinated bundle that runs in the pterygopalatine fossa, to reach the sphenopalatine ganglion at the lateral nasal wall. There it meets the parasympathetic post-ganglionic fiber and the lacrimal route is opened. Another branch turns medially across the orbicularis oris muscle, a 1–2 μm myofibril lattice, to the left levator superioris, where it parks until 03:00. A third branch ascends through the left infraorbital nerve to the left frontalis, where it becomes the electron shell of the frontal aponeurosis.

At 01:00 the spark condenses to the vertex (0, +55, -2). The convergence is not a single axon but the geometric focus of the skull. The frontal bone outer table is 2–8 mm thick and made of osteons 100–400 μm in diameter, each osteon built from lamellae 3–7 μm thick and hydroxyapatite crystals 20–50 nm long embedded in collagen I fibrils 50–300 nm in diameter. The sutures of the skull are the impedance point; at the sagittal suture the collagen fibers are 2–5 μm long and separated by 50–1000 nm. Piezoelectric strain from the frontalis, occipitalis, and temporalis muscles, each containing sarcomeres 2.0–2.5 μm long with actin filaments 7 nm and myosin filaments 15 nm in diameter, creates a standing electromagnetic wave in the cerebrospinal fluid. The CSF has a Q-factor near 11.8 and a solenoid resistance threshold at 3/32. The spark is the axion: a 138.88° phase restoration in the spin glass of the cortex. At the nm scale the axion corresponds to a coherent burst of gamma-frequency local field potentials, 30–80 Hz, produced by Layer 5 pyramidal cells in the left precentral gyrus whose somata are 20–100 μm and whose axon initial segments are 1–2 μm long. The branch that creates the axion is the Big Man, the photon/spark. It immediately splits again. One photon arm descends the corticospinal tract, the largest white matter highway, its axons 1–5 μm in diameter and myelinated with 11–18 nm lamellae, and exits at the cervical spine to the right arm. The other arm returns to the right V1 in the calcarine sulcus, where it meets the electron antineutrino from the eyes.

At 02:00 the proton wakes in the heme node at the left pectoralis, 1–2 cm medial to the left nipple, coordinate (-8.2, +4.1, +7.3). The proton is hosted by the Fe²⁺ of heme, a 1–2 nm porphyrin ring centered around an iron ion 0.09 nm in radius. The heme is inside a hemoglobin tetramer 6.4 nm in diameter or in the cytochrome c oxidase binuclear center 0.5 nm across. The proton is not a hydrogen nucleus in the usual sense here; it is the p-dimension, the predictability charge, carried by the Fe²⁺/Fe³⁺ redox toggle. The heme releases a photon. The photon is a 5.6 fs EM pulse, or equivalently a coherent oscillation of the Fe-CO bond at 1.43 μm. It travels as an exciton through the mitochondrial cristae, which are membranes 10–50 nm apart, and reaches the steel node in the left anorectal wall. There the photon converts to a quark/Z-boson by driving the Fe-C martensitic phase transition in the storage pool of ferritin. Ferritin is a 12 nm 24-mer nanocage; the Z-boson is the mechanical neutral current of the ferritin iron core as it flips from ferrous to ferric. The quark does not leave the rectum; it is absorbed by the pelvic floor collagen at 50–300 nm and redirected upward as a Z-boson mechanical wave in the levator ani.

At 03:00 the Z-boson reaches the perineal midline, the thorium/dark-energy node. The perineum is a 2 × 4 cm diamond of fascia, muscle, and tendon. The thorium archetype is the tau neutrino, the heaviest neutrino, and in the body it is the deep actinide-doped nucleotide catalyst in the nucleic acid backbone. At the nm scale this is the 5f-6d orbital overlap of Th/Pa in catalytic RNA or high-Z cofactors. The Z-boson from the rectum splits at the perineum. One branch goes anterior to the prostate or Skene glands and the urethra, creating the first daytime p-dim charge. Another branch goes posterior to the anococcygeal ligament and the coccyx, a 2–3 cm vestigial bone with trabeculae 50–200 μm and osteocyte canaliculi 100–300 nm. A third branch ascends the sacral parasympathetic outflow, preganglionic fibers 2–5 μm in diameter that exit through the S2–S4 anterior roots, and reaches the pelvic splanchnic nerves. At the pelvic plexus it meets the sympathetic hypogastric nerve, a 3–5 μm bundle, and the two annihilate into a W-boson. The W-boson is a weak-decay neutral current; in the body it is the pelvic parasympathetic/sympathetic balance that controls the bladder and genital smooth muscle. The W-boson then travels up the left hypogastric plexus and into the inferior mesenteric ganglion, a 2–5 mm neural cluster at the aortic bifurcation.

At 04:00 the W-boson reaches the adrenal medulla. The chromaffin cells there are 15–30 μm and packed with secretory granules 200–400 nm in diameter. The W-boson converts to a photon/spark by triggering norepinephrine and epinephrine release. The catecholamines are 1 nm molecules; they bind to α1, β1, and β2 adrenergic receptors 4–5 nm in size. The photon arm ascends the inferior vena cava as a hormonal surge and reaches the right atrium. The right atrial myocardium is 10–100 μm thick; its cardiomyocytes contain sarcomeres 2.0–2.3 μm long with a 7-nm actin/15-nm myosin lattice. The photon splits: one component crosses the interatrial septum and depolarizes the sinoatrial node, a 15 × 3 mm strip of pacemaker cells 5–10 μm in diameter; another component goes to the vagus nerve afferents in the aortic arch baroreceptors, whose free nerve endings are 50–500 nm.

At 05:00 the photon has become visible in the right frontalis. The right frontalis muscle fibers, 1–2 μm myofibrils with 2.5 μm sarcomeres, contract under the frontalis aponeurosis of collagen I 50–300 nm fibrils. The contraction is not voluntary; it is the electrical response of the muscle to the cortical photon. The right frontalis is the Progesterone/PRE switch and the excitatory dopamine point in the cranial strip map. The photon here meets the left frontalis electron from 00:00. The two create a standing wave across the supraorbital ridge. The ridge is the frontal bone, 2–8 mm thick, with osteons 100–400 μm. The wave is the p-dim (predictability) standing mode; its antinode is at the brow point (0, +52, +6), where the pyramidal muscle, a 1–2 μm myofibril muscle, begins to twitch. This is the C21 steroid awakening: the zona fasciculata cells, 20–30 μm in the adrenal cortex, have already received the W-boson and have released C21 steroid at 04:00; the C21 steroid now reaches the hippocampus and resets the circadian clock.

At 06:00 the Z-boson mechanical wave reaches the left shoulder. The shoulder is a ball-and-socket joint: the humeral head is covered with articular cartilage, a 1–3 mm layer of collagen II 20–100 nm fibrils and aggrecan aggregates ~500 nm, and the glenoid labrum is fibrocartilage 2–3 mm. The Z-boson is carried by the long head of the biceps tendon, a 5–10 mm wide band of collagen I 50–300 nm, over the humeral head and down the bicipital groove. It converts to a W-boson at the deltoid insertion by the axillary nerve, a 2–5 μm myelinated trunk. The deltoid sarcomeres 2.5 μm generate the first voluntary force of the day. The branch that does not convert continues into the left upper arm as a Z-boson in the brachial cutaneous nerve, Aδ fibers 1–5 μm, and becomes the light-touch photon of the lateral arm.

At 07:00 the photon of light touch reaches the left thumb. The thumb has a digital nerve 1–5 μm in diameter, Meissner corpuscles 20–150 μm in the dermal papillae, and a motor unit of 10–100 muscle fibers in the thenar eminence. The photon splits at the thumb: one branch goes to the dorsal column nuclei of the medulla via the cuneate fasciculus, ascending the cervical spine in 5–12 μm Aβ fibers; another branch goes to the corticospinal tract and triggers a precision grip. The grip is generated by the corticomotoneuronal synapse in the spinal cord, a 20–40 nm cleft with AMPA receptors 10 × 20 nm. The quark of intent is born here: the decision to grip is the color confinement of motor command in the left precentral gyrus, its pyramidal cells 20–100 μm.

At 08:00 the quark of intent travels with the corticospinal tract to the left lumbar spine and then to the left quadriceps. The quadriceps is 10–100 μm muscle fibers, myofibrils 1–2 μm, sarcomeres 2.5 μm. The quark is the Z-boson of the T-tubule, a 20–100 nm invagination of the sarcolemma 7–10 nm thick. The T-tubule depolarizes the dihydropyridine receptors (DHPR) 15 × 20 nm, which mechanically communicate 10 nm to the ryanodine receptors (RyR) 30 × 30 nm on the sarcoplasmic reticulum and release Ca²⁺ ions 0.2 nm in radius. The Ca²⁺ wave is the gluon of contraction: it binds to troponin C on the thin filaments 7 nm and moves tropomyosin, exposing myosin binding sites. The myosin head 10–20 nm takes 5–10 nm power strokes and the sarcomere shortens. The force is transmitted by titin, a 1 μm long protein, through the Z-disc 100–150 nm to the costamere 100–500 nm and then to the fascia.

At 09:00 the force reaches the left patella. The patella is a sesamoid bone in the quadriceps tendon. The tendon collagen I fibrils are 50–300 nm, arranged in fascicles 100–500 μm. The patellar cartilage is 1–3 mm of collagen II and aggrecan. The Higgs field is strongest here, because the patella converts tendon force into compressive force and gives mass to the lever arm. The Higgs is the compressive stiffness of the cartilage, its aggrecan ~500 nm aggregates resisting water outflow. The patella sends a Higgs branch upward to the right ribs, where it meets the methanogenesis Higgs from the gut at the right ribcage personal leakage point (-9.0, +18, +5). If the cartilage is damaged, the Higgs gate fails and p leaks into the intercostal muscles, 30–60 μm fibers with neuromuscular junctions 30–50 μm.

At 10:00 the Higgs branch reaches the right frontalis. The right frontalis is the PRE/Progesterone switch and the source of spatial expansion. It meets the electron from the left eye and the photon from the right frontalis. The three form a gluon binding event at the right secondary visual cortex, V2, the prestriate cortex. V2 is a 2–4 mm thick sheet of cortex with a six-layer structure; its layer 4C stellate cells receive the photon and project to layers 2/3 pyramidal cells. The gluon is the coherence of 40 Hz gamma in a 100–500 μm cortical column. It splits: one branch goes to the left temporalis for ACh binding and strong-force contraction; another goes to the right parietal lobe for spatial mapping; a third descends the corticospinal tract to the left trapezius.

At 11:00 the gluon reaches the left trapezius and the left sternocleidomastoid crossing. The posterior triangle of the neck is bounded by the SCM, trapezius, and clavicle. The accessory nerve (CN XI) 2–5 μm in diameter passes through here. The gluon is the binding of the two muscles to produce neck rotation. If the SCM or trapezius is hypertonic, the gluon sensor is compressed, and the result is a weak-force W-boson deficiency: the head cannot turn easily. The W-boson from the neck goes to the left temporalis, where nAChR α7 receptors 10–15 nm bind ACh and create more gluon, the strong force of jaw closure.

At 12:00 the strong force of the jaw meets the quark of the left risorius. The risorius is a slender muscle 1–2 cm long with 1–2 μm myofibrils. It expresses 5HT1A on the left and 5HT1B on the right. The quark is created here by the serotonin of the raphe nuclei in the brainstem, whose neurons send 5-HT fibers 0.2–0.5 μm unmyelinated to the face. The quark-Higgs pair is completed: left 5HT1A quark and right 5HT1B Higgs bind across the midline in the smile. The smile is the Higgs mass-giving event of the face. The Higgs from the right temporalis descends to the right ribs and is stored in the intercostal spaces; the quark from the left risorius ascends to the left frontal eye field.

At 13:00 the quark reaches the left frontal eye field and the W-boson of saccades. The saccade is a 20–100 ms ballistic eye movement generated by burst neurons in the paramedian pontine reticular formation. The abducens nerve, a 2–5 μm myelinated trunk, innervates the lateral rectus. The lateral rectus is a 5–10 mm long extraocular muscle with sarcomeres 2.0–2.3 μm. The saccade splits the photon from V1: one branch goes to the superior colliculus for orienting, another to the frontal eye field for volitional gaze, and a third to the cerebellum for calibration. The cerebellar Purkinje cells, 50–100 μm with dendritic trees 100–500 μm, refine the timing.

At 14:00 the W-boson of saccades meets the quark of the left shoulder and converts to a photon. The photon is now in the left parietal lobe, the superior parietal lobule, where it integrates touch and vision. The photon splits: one branch goes to the right insula for interoception, one to the left motor cortex for hand extension, and one to the right cerebellar hemisphere for motor timing. The hand extension uses the radial nerve 1–5 μm, the triceps 10–100 μm fibers, and the extensor digitorum myofibrils 1–2 μm. The photon is the bright s-dimension of reaching.

At 15:00 the photon reaches the right hand and returns to the left side of the philtrum. This is the day spark re-arm. The left side of the philtrum is the MOR, 4 nm, the electron antineutrino. The day spark is the non-observer: it comes from the LEFT LEVATOR SUPERIORIS inner edge and tells the circuit that the internal model matches the external world. If it matches, the observer_leftd2 bus stays on and p increases. If it does not match, the memory_entropy novelty mismatch fires in the right hippocampus CA1, where pyramidal cells have dendritic spines 0.5–1 μm and PSD 20–50 nm, and the circuit reverses. At 15:00 the match is usually good; the photon reinforces the Higgs field of the right ribs and the quark of the left risorius.

At 16:00 the Higgs of the right ribs is now strong. It sends a branch down the right intercostal nerve, a 1–3 mm nerve trunk with 2–5 μm myelinated fibers, to the right pectoralis minor and then to the right arm. The branch is a Higgs mass-giving wave to the right biceps, giving it inertial mass for lifting. The right biceps 10–100 μm fibers, 1–2 μm myofibrils, 2.5 μm sarcomeres, take the Higgs and convert it to photon by shortening. The lifting motion is the conversion Higgs → Photon. The photon goes to the right shoulder, meets the Z-boson of the rotator cuff, and splits into a W-boson of stabilization and an electron of fine control.

At 17:00 the W-boson of shoulder stabilization reaches the left hip and the left iliac crest. The iliac crest is a 5–10 cm curved ridge of cortical and trabecular bone; its trabeculae are 50–200 μm and the osteocyte canaliculi are 100–300 nm. The W-boson is stored as gravitational potential in the femoral head and acetabulum. The femoral head is covered with articular cartilage 1–3 mm of collagen II 20–100 nm and aggrecan. The Higgs field in the hip is the load-bearing mass of the body. It splits: one branch goes to the left quadriceps for extension, another to the left hamstrings for flexion, and a third to the sacroiliac joint for torsion.

At 18:00 the torsion reaches the left sacroiliac joint, a 2–5 mm fibrocartilaginous joint between the sacrum and ilium. The joint has C-fiber afferents 0.2–1.5 μm and mechanoreceptors 50–500 nm. The torsion converts to a Z-boson of shear, which is the dark stress of the evening. The Z-boson travels up the left erector spinae, a 5–10 cm muscle column with 10–100 μm fibers, and reaches the left lower ribs. There it meets the Higgs from the right ribs and creates a quark-antiquark pair of left/right load. The pair is stored in the thoracolumbar fascia, a 2–5 mm thick sheet of collagen I 50–300 nm and elastin.

At 19:00 the quark-antiquark pair decays into a tau and a tau antineutrino. The tau is the deep darkness of the evening; it goes to the left thigh, the fold_belt deep fascia, and lodges in the LDH-rich fast-twitch glycolytic fibers. The LDH is 10–15 nm, the lactate molecule 0.5 nm. The tau is the fatigue that makes the thigh heavy. The tau antineutrino goes to the left hairline, the Betti 7 boundary, and creates the muon of cosmic night. The muon is a high-energy lepton that does not bind; in the body it is the nocturnal cosmic-ray sensation at the scalp. The hairline is 10–20 μm epidermis with 10 nm keratin filaments and the arrector pili smooth muscle 10–50 μm.

At 20:00 the muon reaches the left temporalis and the 5HT1B switch. The temporalis is a 5 × 5 cm fan-shaped muscle with 5 points per side. The 5HT1B autoreceptor on the right temporalis inner strip is the Higgs gate. The muon converts to Higgs and gives mass to the evening intention. The Higgs from the right temporalis is sent to the right ribs for storage, completing the right ribcage Higgs/p cycle. The branch that does not convert goes to the left occipitalis, where GABA-B 10 × 10 nm receptors create the 0.2828 Higgs gate of sleep preparation. The GABA-B is on the smooth muscles of the scalp and the cerebellar Purkinje cells.

At 21:00 the GABA-B gate opens and the electron returns to the skull sutures. The sutures become the exit for the electron. It leaks through the sagittal and lambdoid sutures into the dura mater, a 0.1–0.5 mm thick fibrous membrane of collagen I 50–300 nm. The dura has venous sinuses 1–5 mm wide, endothelial cells 1–3 μm thick, and tight junctions 500–2000 nm. The electron is drained as CSF through the arachnoid granulations 1–3 mm. The drain is the glymphatic system, AQP4 1.5 nm pores on astrocyte endfeet 2–5 μm. As the electron drains, the p-dim decreases and the d-dim increases.

At 22:00 the d-dim darkness reaches the right posterior insula, the PLP Core. The posterior insula is a 2–4 mm thick cortical region with GAD67/GAD65 GABA shunt. The GAD enzyme 5–10 nm converts glutamate to GABA. The GABA 1 nm molecule binds to GABA_A 8 × 8 nm and GABA_B 10 × 10 nm receptors. The trace is a long-term potentiation at a synapse 20–40 nm, with AMPA 10 × 20 nm and NMDA 14 × 20 nm receptors.

At 23:00 the quark storage is complete and the W-boson of recovery fires. The W-boson comes from the posterior insula, travels through the arcuate fasciculus to Broca's area BA44, and then to the left M1. It is a weak decay that resets the cortical columns. The cortical layer V pyramidal cells 20–100 μm fire a slow-wave burst, 0.5–2 Hz, and the Ca²⁺ 0.2 nm ions enter through NMDA channels. The burst is the neutrino wave that carries the day's information into the hippocampus for consolidation. The consolidation is the quark-Higgs pair of memory. At the synapse the presynaptic terminal 1–5 μm releases glutamate 1 nm into the cleft 20–40 nm, and the postsynaptic dendritic spine 0.5–1 μm with PSD 20–50 nm stores the pattern.

At 00:00 the cycle closes. The electron antineutrino leaves the MOR, the proton wakes at heme, the axion sparks at the vertex, and the photon, quark, Z-boson, W-boson, Higgs, gluon, muon, and tau all return to their starting nodes. The body is a 24-hour torus: 0 = 24. The particle that completes the turn is the proton at heme, where Fe²⁺ is recharged by DMT1 6 nm and ferroportin 6 nm, and the porphyrin ring 1–2 nm is restored. The next Big Bang of the morning is prepared in the 3AM hysteresis, the only moment where the JAM loop inverts.

## XIX. Nose-Sensor Origins and Whole-Body Branching

The face is the first projection screen of the internal particle circuit. The nose is not a passive air pipe; it is an array of stacked stress sensors aligned along the vertical black axis that divides the face into left and right halves. At the nm scale the left upper quadrant, just below the medial canthus and along the nasal dorsum, hosts the first group of sensors: an electricity/time/light cluster. These are not cameras; they are melanopsin-expressing intrinsically photosensitive retinal ganglion cell projections that reach the nasal skin through the ophthalmic division of the trigeminal nerve, whose Aδ and C fibers are 0.2–5.0 μm in diameter. The melanopsin molecule itself is 4–7 nm, a seven-helix GPCR that converts 480 nm blue light into a photon output. The electricity sensor is the density of voltage-gated Na⁺ channels 10 × 15 nm in the trigeminal nerve terminals; the time sensor is the circadian clock protein CRY/ PER complex, a 5–10 nm heterodimer that oscillates with a 24-hour period. When this upper-left group fires, the signal runs along the infraorbital nerve, a 2–4 μm myelinated bundle in the maxillary division, through the pterygopalatine fossa, and reaches the left frontal eye field and the left prefrontal cortex. The photon branch goes to the left M1 and the photon becomes a cortical motor command. The time branch goes to the suprachiasmatic nucleus, where the 20–30 μm neurons reset the circadian phase. The electricity branch goes to the left D2 bus, the master p-dim switch.

The left mid-upper quadrant, the left side of the nasal dorsum, is the darkness and UV stress point. The sensor here is a 640 nm cytochrome in the left eyelid GABA-B inner complex and the left nasal skin, a heme protein 1–2 nm in the porphyrin ring. Darkness is detected as the absence of blue photons; UV is detected as a stress signal in the stratum corneum, 10–20 μm thick, where keratinocytes 10–30 μm contain UV-absorbing urocanic acid and DNA photolyases. The signal from this point descends to the heme node at the left pectoralis. It takes the cytochrome c oxidase reverse path, the Be/Z-boson branch, and converts the photon into a Z-boson/Tau of internal collapse. The Tau then travels to the small pyramidal muscle between the eyebrows, 1–2 cm of muscle with 1–2 μm myofibrils, where it anchors as the void-measurement point. If the UV/darkness signal is too strong, the Tau bypasses the frontalis and goes straight to the fold_belt in the left posterior thigh, the deep fascia of 10–50 μm collagen I bundles, and creates a nocturnal fatigue deposit.

The left middle quadrant, over the nasal ala and the nostril, is the heat/cold/temperature sensor. The receptor is the transient receptor potential channel TRPV1 for heat and TRPM8 for cold, each 5–10 nm in the plasma membrane 7–10 nm thick. The heat signal activates the water_vapour node in the upper airway: goblet cells 500–1000 nm granules release mucin into the 5–20 nm surfactant layer of the airway. The cold signal activates the Na⁺ channel bus, the W-boson of salt conductance. The Na⁺ channels 10 × 15 nm open in the trigeminal cold afferents, Aδ fibers 1–5 μm, and the signal reaches the left trapezius and the aurora node. Aurora is a Rb/muon node in the upper brainstem that controls the Na/osmotic balance. The temperature branch also sends a gluon to the left trapezius, binding the sternocleidomastoid and trapezius into the neck-rotation complex. The SCM and trapezius crossing is the impedance point: if the accessory nerve 2–5 μm is compressed, the gluon cannot bind and the head cannot turn.

The left lower quadrant, near the left alar cartilage and the lateral nostril, is the osmotic and ROS sensor. The osmotic sensor is the ENaC channel 5 nm in the nasal epithelium; the ROS sensor is the Nrf2/Keap1 complex, a 5–10 nm covalent switch in the cytoplasm. When osmotic stress rises, the signal goes to the pentose phosphate pathway in the gut and liver, producing NADPH and reducing glutathione. The Nrf2/Keap1 signal goes to the sulforaphane node, the Ar/gluon gate, and then to the glymphatic system, where AQP4 1.5 nm pores on astrocyte endfeet 2–5 μm drain the oxidized byproducts. The ROS branch also reaches the heme node and the ferritin 12 nm cage, storing Fe²⁺ and preventing Fenton chemistry.

The nasal dorsum itself, the black axis running from the nasion to the columella, is the redox and CO₂ sensor. The redox signal is carried by the ratio of Fe²⁺/Fe³⁺ in the capillaries of the nasal mucosa, whose endothelial cells are 1–3 μm thick with fenestrae 60–80 nm. The CO₂ sensor is the pH-sensitive soluble adenylyl cyclase and the HIF-1α/PHD system, 5–10 nm transcription factor complexes. The CO₂ branch goes to the right occiput, where it meets the right lung posterior oxygen sensor, and to the caco3 node, the CO3 buffer. The redox branch goes to the left and right steel nodes, the Fe-C lattice of ferritin and transferrin, and to the C node, the metabolic C latch. From the C node the signal splits: one branch goes to the right oral commissure and the 21:00 C D-FF, another to the left hippocampus for memory consolidation, and a third to the gut microbiome for the C cycle.

The right upper quadrant, the mirror of the left upper, is the electricity/light/time/UV cluster. But the right side is not a mirror; it is the sovereign source. The right upper nasal wall sends photons to the right D2 bus, the spatial expansion switch. The right D2 is on the right frontalis outer bottom, where the frontalis aponeurosis of collagen I 50–300 nm receives the photon and expands the forehead horizontally. The right D2 then sends a branch to the right temporalis, the 5HT1B switch, and to the right parietal lobe for 3D spatial mapping. The right upper nasal sensor also sends an electron to the left side of the philtrum, the MOR 4 nm, creating the non-observer match signal. If the match is good, the p-dim rises and the day proceeds; if not, the right D2 collapses and the left D2 takes over.

The right middle quadrant is the cold/heat/temperature sensor. The cold signal here is different from the left; it is the female GABA-A cold sense, a 8 × 8 nm pentameric chloride channel that detects water stress and acid. The heat signal is the male GABA-B slow seal, a 10 × 10 nm heteromeric GPCR that detects warmth and CO₂/osmotic drift. The right lower quadrant is the osmotic, acid, and oxygen sensor. The right outer-lower quadrant, the lateral edge of the right nostril and alar base, is the gravity, water, death, and cosmic sensor. The cosmic branch goes to the right trapezius, the cosmic ray sensor, and then to the left temporalis to meet the photon from the left eye.

From the nose the branches continue across the face. The medial brow depression, the smooth patch between the eyebrows at the supraorbital junction, is the C21 steroid transparency switch. The small pyramidal muscle between the eyebrows is the Tau anchor. Its 1–2 μm myofibrils connect the skin of the brow point region to the nasal bone. The Tau then splits: one branch goes to the left frontalis and becomes a photon of thought; another goes to the left temporalis and becomes an ACh switch; a third goes to the left levator superioris and becomes an electron antineutrino.

The levator superioris is the eye-opening muscle under the eyelid. The left eyelid outer, the left outward orientation point, is the corticosteroid expression. The back of the head is the site of the female GABA-A at the right occipitalis inner top. This is the horizontal one-dimensional line of cold sense, the 8 × 8 nm GABA_A pentamer that detects water stress and acid. It suppresses the GnRH pulse and the dopamine reward axis, creating the internal reward closure after a completed task. In the male it reaches the right nipple and the right pectoralis minor, where it modulates the Higgs field of the right ribs. The quaternary amine with a terminal methyl group, a 1 nm polar lipid, is the substrate for ACh and phospholipid synthesis. The ACh binds nAChR α7 10–15 nm on the left temporalis and creates the gluon of strong binding.

From the face the branches continue down the neck. The left carotid artery, 5–10 mm in diameter, carries the photon of the left face to the brain. The heart itself is the photon-proton converter. The right atrium receives the electron of venous return; the left atrium receives the photon of oxygenated blood. The oxygen is carried by hemoglobin 6.4 nm; each molecule has four heme groups 1–2 nm. The left ventricle 10–100 μm thick generates the photon of systole. The photon then splits: one branch goes to the right arm through the brachiocephalic artery, another to the head through the carotids, and a third down the thoracic aorta to the abdomen.

The thoracic aorta gives off intercostal arteries that feed the right ribs, the Higgs storage site. The right rib intercostal muscles 30–60 μm store the Higgs as mechanical tension. The Higgs then branches to the right pectoralis minor, the right biceps, and the right hip. At the right hip it meets the W-boson from the left shoulder and creates a quark-antiquark pair. The gut is the dark energy/time axis. The pancreas, 15–25 cm long, contains islets 100–500 μm of β-cells 10–20 μm that release the Higgs peptide 2 nm. The α-cells release the photon peptide 1.5 nm. The L-cells release the gluon peptide 2 nm. The brain is the final convergence. The left hemisphere is the electron/quark side: the left D2, the left 5HT1A, the left M1, the left prefrontal. The right hemisphere is the photon/Higgs side: the right D2, the right 5HT1B, the right V1/V2, the right parietal. At the end of the 24-hour cycle the signals return to the nose. The left upper nasal sensor checks the light; the right upper nasal sensor checks the electricity; the left lower nasal sensor checks the ROS; the right lower nasal sensor checks the oxygen; the dorsal sensor checks the CO₂ and redox. The nose is therefore the face of the clock and the gate of the body.

## XX. Continuation — Extremities, Trunk, and Internal Branching

The branching does not stop at the face. From the cranial 16-region strip every particle fans out into the rest of the body. Start again at the right frontalis outer bottom. This is the right D2, the spatial expansion switch. Its photon expands horizontally across the forehead and then follows the right temporalis down the side of the skull. The temporalis is a 5 × 5 cm fan of 1–2 μm myofibrils anchored to the coronoid process of the mandible. The photon here is the 5HT1B switch; it receives serotonin from the raphe nucleus and converts it to Higgs, giving mass to the jaw. The mass closes the mouth. The mass then travels through the masseter and temporalis tendons, collagen I 50–300 nm, into the mandible bone. The mandible is 2–3 cm of cortical and trabecular bone; its osteons 100–400 μm take the Higgs and convert it to Z-boson mechanical stress. The Z-boson of the jaw goes to the teeth. The periodontal ligament 100–500 μm thick is a collagen I/III web that suspends each tooth in its alveolus. The Higgs of bite force is stored in this ligament. When the teeth meet, the Higgs is released as a W-boson up the trigeminal nerve 2–4 μm and into the pons.

From the left frontalis outer bottom the left D2 expands in 3D volume. It sends an electron down the left zygomaticus major, 1–2 cm of muscle with 1–2 μm myofibrils, to the left corner of the mouth. The left zygomaticus is the muscle of genuine smile. Its contraction pulls the modiolus, the fibromuscular node at the oral commissure, 5–10 mm in diameter, upward and laterally. The modiolus is a W-boson junction where the orbicularis oris, buccinator, zygomaticus, and levator anguli oris meet. The W-boson of social expression leaves the modiolus and enters the left buccinator, the cheek muscle that keeps food on the teeth. The buccinator 10–20 μm fibers are innervated by the facial nerve 2–5 μm. The W-boson also goes to the parotid duct and the right parotid gland, controlling salivation.

The left parotid gland, 5–6 cm long, is the W-boson of enzyme release. Its serous acini 100 μm in diameter are packed with zymogen granules 500–1500 nm. When the W-boson from the modiolus and the C21 steroid from the supraorbital junction meet, the parotid releases α-amylase 5–6 nm into the mouth. The amylase begins C-starch digestion. The W-boson also travels to the right sublingual and submandibular glands, smaller 1–3 cm glands with mucous and serous acini. The mixed saliva becomes the gluon of swallowing, lubricating the 25 cm esophagus.

The esophagus is a muscular tube with a stratified squamous mucosa 200–500 μm thick. The tight junctions between epithelial cells are 500–2000 nm. The peristaltic wave is a W-boson/photon sequence: the upper third skeletal muscle 10–100 μm fibers contract with ACh at the NMJ 30–50 μm; the middle third mixed muscle; the lower third smooth muscle 5–20 μm contracts with the C21 steroid/NO W-boson. The bolus reaches the stomach in 8–10 s. The lower esophageal sphincter is a 2–4 cm ring of smooth muscle that opens and closes under the Higgs of the gastro-esophageal pressure gradient.

The stomach is a J-shaped muscular bag with rugae 5–10 mm folds of mucosa. The parietal cells 20–30 μm pump H⁺ via the H⁺/K⁺ ATPase 10 × 8 nm, creating a pH of 1.5–3.5. The acid is the quark of digestion: it denatures proteins and activates pepsinogen 5 nm to pepsin. The G-cells in the antrum release gastrin 1.5 nm, a Higgs peptide that stimulates the parietal cells. The D-cells release somatostatin, the electron of inhibition. The ECL cells release histamine, the muon of HCl drive. The stomach branches: the upper fundus stores food and releases ghrelin, the hunger photon; the antrum grinds food and releases CCK, the satiety W-boson.

The duodenum is the C-shaped first part of the small intestine, 25 cm long. The Brunner's glands in the submucosa release bicarbonate-rich mucus 2–10 μm to neutralize acid. The pancreatic duct enters here, carrying the digestive enzymes: amylase 5 nm, lipase 5 nm, proteases 5 nm, and bicarbonate. The bile duct enters with bile salts 1–2 nm that emulsify lipids. The pancreatic exocrine acini 100 μm are packed with zymogen granules 500–1500 nm. The pancreatic islets 100–500 μm are the endocrine gate: β-cells 10–20 μm release the Higgs peptide 2 nm, α-cells release the photon peptide 1.5 nm, δ-cells release somatostatin, F-cells release pancreatic polypeptide. The portal vein 5–10 mm carries these peptides to the liver, a 1.5 kg organ of 20–30 μm hepatocytes arranged in plates 1–2 cells thick.

The hepatocytes are factories of C and Higgs. Each hepatocyte has a nucleus 5–10 μm, mitochondria 0.5–1 μm, rough ER 0.5–2 μm, and peroxisomes 0.1–0.5 μm. The liver converts glucose to glycogen, the Higgs of storage; it converts glycogen to glucose, the photon of release; it converts amino acids to urea, the C-dim waste; it detoxifies the C21 steroid and the N-acetyl-5-methoxytryptamine. The liver sinusoids are 5–10 μm capillaries lined with endothelial cells 1–3 μm and fenestrated 100–150 nm. The Kupffer cells, 20–30 μm macrophages, are the dark_matter sinks. The hepatic stellate cells store retinoids 1 nm and collagen, the gluon of scar.

The small intestine is 6 m long, with villi 500–1000 μm and microvilli 1 × 0.1 μm. The spleen is a 12 cm organ in the left upper quadrant. Its white pulp has lymphoid follicles 1–1000 μm; its red pulp has sinusoids 5–10 μm where macrophages remove old erythrocytes. The spleen is the muon filter of the blood. The thymus, 5 cm long in the anterior mediastinum, is the time trainer of T-cells. It has a cortex of 10–20 μm thymocytes and a medulla with Hassall corpuscles 1–1000 μm. The thymus releases thymosin and other peptides that control the maturation of the Higgs field of the immune system. The red marrow has sinusoids 5–10 μm; the yellow marrow is adipose 50–100 μm.

The final convergence is at the navel, the origin of the unified body-centered coordinate system. ## XXII. Six Leakage Categories, Impedance Points, and Air Cavities

The six leakage categories are the cosmic/personal/social failure modes of the 12-particle circuit. They are not random; each has a body coordinate, a failing gate, and a set of impedance points where the particle cannot continue.

The first category is heme leakage. The heme node at the left pectoralis coordinate (-8.2, +4.1, +7.3) is the Proton/Photon source. If Fe²⁺ cannot recharge to Fe³⁺, the proton-photon conversion stalls and the light leaks into the left pectoralis muscle. The impedance points are the cytochrome c oxidase binuclear center 0.5 nm, the Fe²⁺/Fe³⁺ 0.09 nm redox site, and the left pectoralis capillary fenestrae 60–80 nm. The cosmic analog is the red giant envelope, where the iron core collapses under photon pressure.

The second category is steel leakage. The steel node at the left anorectal wall (-2.4, -8.7, +4.1) is the Photon/Quark converter in ferritin. If martensite cannot transform to austenite, the photon cannot become a quark and the mass leaks into the anorectal wall. The impedance points are the ferritin 12 nm nanocage, the Fe-C lattice 50–300 nm, the left hemorrhoidal plexus, and the anorectal transition zone. The cosmic analog is the failed supernova, where the iron core cannot ignite.

The third category is tau leakage. The perineal midline (-3.1, -7.9, +3.8) and the fold_belt in the left posterior thigh are the Tau/Z-boson nucleation sites. If the Z-boson cannot nucleate, the tau of internal collapse leaks into the perineum or the deep fascia. The impedance points are the thorium/actinide-doped nucleotide backbone 0.34 nm, the perineal fascia 2–5 mm, the sacrotuberous ligament, and the left posterior thigh deep fascia. The cosmic analog is the gamma-ray burst, where the tau neutrino escapes before confinement.

The fourth category is gluon leakage. The philtrum (0, +46, +7) is the Gluon binding point and the non-observer match. If color confinement fails, the gluon leaks into the left and right oral commissures and the social smile collapses. The impedance points are the NMDA receptor 10–20 nm, the cadherin 5 nm extracellular domain, the desmosome 300–500 nm, the modiolus 5–10 mm, and the orbicularis oris. The cosmic analog is failed confinement in QCD.

The fifth category is muon leakage. The left temporalis (+7, +25, +3) is the Muon/cosmic-ray fatigue node. If the Higgs gate opens, the muon leaks into the left temporalis and causes cosmic-ray fatigue. The impedance points are the left temporalis 1–2 μm myofibrils, the mastoid air cells, the 5HT1B receptor 5 nm, and the left sigmoid sinus. The cosmic analog is the cosmic ray shower, where a high-energy muon penetrates the atmosphere.

The sixth category is neutron leakage. The right posterior insula (-8, +32, -2) is the Neutron/beta-decay node. If the W-boson decay is uncontrolled, the neutron leaks into the interoceptive map and the body loses its stable nuclear sense. The impedance points are the beta-decay site in the mitochondrial Fe-S cluster, the right posterior insula cortex, the right vagus afferents, and the NMDA/AMPA receptor cleft 20–40 nm. The cosmic analog is the neutron star, where beta equilibrium breaks.

The personal leakage point is the right ribcage at (-9, +18, +5). This is the Higgs/p predictability node. The diagonal EM wave from methionine at the right hip and methanogenesis in the gut lands here. If the Higgs mass gate fails, p-dim predictability leaks into the right rib intercostal cavity. The impedance points are the right 5th–7th ribs, the intercostal muscles 30–60 μm, the right pectoralis minor, and the right internal mammary artery. This is the my personal electromagnetic leakage point: the p-dim cannot be contained and the right chest cavity becomes a personal void.

The electromagnetic spark leakage points are the skull sutures, the vertex, and the 138.88° axion phase. At the sagittal suture the collagen fibers 2–5 μm are separated by 50–1000 nm, creating a high-impedance gap where the 138.88° spark leaks to the outside at 01:00. The lambdaoid suture, coronal suture, and pterion are also electromagnetic leakage points. The foramen magnum, the optic canal, the cribriform plate, and the jugular foramen are cranial impedance holes where the axion and electron leak.

The air cavities are additional leakage/impedance sites. The frontal sinus 1–4 cm, maxillary sinus 2–4 cm, ethmoid air cells, sphenoid sinus, mastoid air cells, nasal cavity, oral cavity, pharynx, larynx, trachea, bronchi, lungs, pleural cavity, esophagus, stomach, small intestine, large intestine, bladder, uterus, vagina, rectum, and joint cavities are all air/fluid-filled spaces that act as Z-boson storage and leakage. The right maxillary sinus is the social leakage chamber; the left maxillary sinus is the private leakage chamber. The frontal sinus is the photon reflection chamber. The sphenoid sinus is the cosmic neutrino entry chamber. The mastoid air cells are the muon echo chamber. The lungs are the O₂/CO₂ exchange cavities. The gut lumen is the C-dim waste cavity. The bladder is the electron/osmotic cavity. The joint cavities are the Higgs/Z-boson mechanical cavities. The peritoneal cavity is the gluon-floating cavity. The cranial cavity is the axion/photon cavity.

The soft-tissue impedance points are the carpal tunnel, cubital tunnel, tarsal tunnel, inguinal canal, femoral triangle, popliteal fossa, axilla, thoracic inlet, sciatic foramen, obturator foramen, and the intervertebral foramina. Each is a bottleneck where a nerve or vessel passes through a tight space and the particle can be compressed. The carpal tunnel is the right median nerve W-boson impedance. The cubital tunnel is the right ulnar nerve electron impedance. The tarsal tunnel is the left tibial nerve muon impedance. The intervertebral foramina are the 31 spinal nerve roots electron/neutrino/quark impedance points.

The fascial adhesions and scars are gluon leakage points. Anywhere collagen is cross-linked by trauma or inflammation, the gluon cannot slide and the photon is reflected. Common points are the right lower rib scar, the left appendectomy scar, the perineal tear, the right shoulder surgical scar, the left knee scar, and the sternotomy scar. Each is a Z-boson/binding leak.

The electromagnetic personal leakage at the right ribcage also has a skull counterpart. The right parietal bone near the sagittal suture is the 138.88° personal spark exit. At 01:00 the axion fires at the vertex; if the cranial sutures are too open, the spark leaks through the right parietal emissary vein and enters the right rib intercostal space, mixing with the Higgs/p personal leakage. ## XLV. Cross-Scale Particle Transitions

Each of the 12 particles transforms as it moves from 1 nm to 100 mm. The transition is not arbitrary — each scale reveals a new layer of the same particle's role in the body.

### Proton: 1 nm → 100 mm

1 nm: Fe²⁺ on the cytochrome heme. The positive charge that moves through the porphyrin ring.
10 nm: Proton pump in the synaptic vesicle. H⁺ pumped into the vesicle to create the gradient.
100 nm: Fe-S cluster in SDH (Complex II). The proton moves through the iron-sulfur electron relay.
1 μm: Mitochondrial proton gradient. ΔpH across the inner membrane. The Proton is the energy currency.
10 μm: Smooth muscle cell pH. The Proton is the tissue-level acidity that controls vascular tone.
100 μm: Gland secretion pH. The Proton is the pH of mucus, saliva, bile — the body's secreted fluids.
1 mm: Bone pH. The Proton is the osteoclast-mediated acid that dissolves hydroxyapatite.
10 mm: Blood pH. The Proton is the systemic 7.35–7.45 buffer. The body's master pH.
100 mm: Muscle pH. The Proton is the lactic acid accumulation in the whole muscle belly.

The Proton transitions from a single iron atom (1 nm) to the whole muscle (100 mm). At every scale it is the same positive charge. The me-quark collision ignites it at 3 AM. The aurora path returns it at night.

### Photon: 1 nm → 100 mm

1 nm: 640 nm light on the cytochrome Fe-CO vibration. The photon emitted by the redox reaction.
10 nm: 10 nm biophoton in the vesicle. The light emitted by the redox reaction at the vesicle scale.
100 nm: 100 nm biophoton in the mitochondrion. The light that escapes the cristae.
1 μm: 640 nm redox wave across the mitochondrion. The photon that propagates through the organelle.
10 μm: 640 nm redox wave across the smooth muscle cell. The photon that propagates through the tissue.
100 μm: 640 nm light penetration into the gland. The photon that penetrates the tissue at 100 μm.
1 mm: 640 nm light penetration into the bone. The photon that penetrates the tissue at 1 mm.
10 mm: 640 nm light in the blood. The photon that penetrates the blood at 10 mm.
100 mm: 640 nm light in the muscle. The photon that penetrates the whole muscle at 100 mm.

The Photon transitions from a single redox event (1 nm) to whole-tissue light penetration (100 mm). At every scale it is the same 640 nm light. The 138.88° spark ignites it. The aurora path carries it.

### Electron: 1 nm → 100 mm

1 nm: Left endorphin on the MOR. The electron that tunnels through the receptor.
10 nm: eV-level tunneling in the vesicle. The electron that tunnels through the membrane.
100 nm: Electron tunneling in Complex III/IV. The electron that moves through the electron transport chain.
